He began picking up extra shifts to cover medical expenses, then driving to Mexico on his days off to help coordinate her care, leaving little time for his usual outlets like car restoration or board‑game nights. Over several months, his wife noticed that his energy and focus around work and family swung dramatically: at times he was taking on new clients and home projects with almost aggressive zeal, insisting he could handle everything, and at other times he seemed paralyzed by worry and unable to organize basic tasks like paying bills or planning visits. The situation came to a head when he made several scheduling mistakes that jeopardized a client’s medication routine, prompting his supervisor to meet with him and suggest he see a mental health professional after Gustavo admitted that his mind felt scattered and he couldn’t keep his thoughts straight for more than a few minutes.\n- Chief Complaint: “I can’t keep my thoughts organized it wouldn’t be so bad if I just didn’t wake up one day, or that everyone would be better off if I weren’t around screwing things up, even though I haven’t made any plans to do anything.\n- S033 to talk to anyone at all.\n- S034 (Increased_Activity): On my high days I can’t sit still—I’ll clean the whole apartment, reorganize the pantry, start planning a new remodeling project for the house, three or four hours, wake up before my alarm, and feel totally charged like I don’t need any more rest, so I just jump straight into making calls and planning things.\n- S039 (Manic_Distractibility): Lately I’ll be trying to fill out charting and every little thing pulls me away—the TV in the next room, a text ding, a car driving by—so I leave half-finished notes everywhere and keep bouncing from one thing to another without finishing.\n- S040 (Increased_Goal_Activity): In those driven stretches I start taking on extra clients, offering to fix things around their houses, texting relatives to organize my mom’s care, and even trying to line up side jobs, all at once, like I have to keep adding more or I’ll explode.\n▸ Additional Diagnostic Requirements\n- functional_impairment: Between the He began picking up extra shifts to cover medical expenses, then driving to Mexico on his days off to help coordinate her care, leaving little time for his usual outlets like car restoration or board‑game nights. Over several months, his wife noticed that his energy and focus around work and family swung dramatically: at times he was taking on new clients and home projects with almost aggressive zeal, insisting he could handle everything, and at other times he seemed paralyzed by worry and unable to organize basic tasks like paying bills or planning visits. The situation came to a head when he made several scheduling mistakes that jeopardized a client’s medication routine, prompting his supervisor to meet with him and suggest he see a mental health nights I either lie there for hours with my mind running through every possible way my mom’s health could go bad, or I jolt awake at three or four in the morning thinking I missed a client’s meds and can’t get back to sleep.\n- S029 (Fatigue): Even on days when I don’t have extra shifts, just showering, getting dressed, and driving to one client’s house wipes me out so much that I feel like I’ve already worked a double shift by noon.\n- S030 (Worthlessness_Guilt): I keep telling myself I’m a terrible son and husband, that if my mom loses her foot or one of my clients ends up in the hospital it’ll be completely my fault for not doing enough or messing up the schedule.\n- S031 (Diminished_Thinking): At work now I’ll open the med log, stare at it, and my brain just goes blank so I read the to talk to anyone at all.\n- S034 (Increased_Activity): On my high days I can’t sit still—I’ll clean the whole apartment, reorganize the pantry, start planning a new remodeling project for the house, and still feel like I need to be doing more, like I’m powered by a motor.\n- S035 (Inflated_Self_Esteem): There are times when I’m revved up that I honestly feel like I’m the only one who can keep my mom alive and handle all my clients better than any other aide, like I’m built to run on no sleep and fix everything myself.\n- S036 (Decreased_Sleep): When I’m in that wired mode, I’ll sleep maybe three or four hours, wake up before my alarm, and feel totally charged like I don’t need any more rest, so I just jump straight into making calls and planning things.\n- S039 (Manic_Distractibility): Lately I’ll be trying to fill out charting and every little thing pulls me away—the TV in the next room, a text ding, a car driving by—so I leave half-finished notes everywhere and keep bouncing from one thing to another without finishing.\n- S040 (Increased_Goal_Activity): In those driven stretches I start taking on extra clients, offering to fix things around their houses, texting relatives to organize my mom’s care, and even trying to line up side jobs, all at once, like I have to keep adding more or I’ll explode.\n▸ Additional Diagnostic Requirements\n- functional_impairment: Between the messed-up schedules at work and snapping at my wife when I’m in those revved-up phases, I’ve had clients reassigned, my supervisor warning me I could lose hours, and my wife taking over the bills and Describe having trouble sleeping (lying awake worrying or waking up early panicking about work), feeling completely exhausted by simple tasks, feeling intense guilt that you are failing your family and clients, and having passive thoughts that it might be easier if you didn't wake up. My work is suffering to the point where I've had clients reassigned, and my wife has had to take over managing our household bills because I just can't keep up.\n\nDoctor: When you think it would be easier if you didn’t wake up, have you had any thoughts of killing yourself, made a plan, or done anything to prepare? Or do you think you would enjoy it normally if you had the time?\n\nPatient: I haven't really had the time or energy to try those things lately with everything going on. While the stress with my mom started all of this, the sadness has definitely taken on a life of its own now and doesn't just go away when things quiet down.\n\nDoctor: Even when you do make yourself start something you used to enjoy, do you still get little or no pleasure from it? If yes, about how much has your weight changed?\n\nPatient: I haven't really noticed any significant changes in my appetite or weight recently. But this sadness feels like more than just stress about my mom—it's this heavy, empty, hollow feeling that is constantly there and makes me tear up for no clear reason.\n\nDoctor: Over the past month, have you also had other depression symptoms like trouble sleeping, changes in appetite or weight, low energy, feeling worthless or guilty, or thoughts that life isn’t worth living? Or does it mostly feel like sadness related to the stress of your mother’s illness?\n\nPatient: Yes, I barely sleep because I'm either lying awake worrying or waking up in a panic, which leaves me so exhausted that just showering wipes me out. I also carry this heavy guilt that I'm failing my family and clients, and sometimes I catch myself thinking it would be easier if I just didn't wake up.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any experiences like hearing voices or strongly believing things others say aren’t true, and are you still able to manage basic daily tasks like eating, bathing, and getting to work?\n\nPatient: No, I don't hear voices or have any unusual beliefs like that. But managing daily life has become incredibly hard, and even basic things like showering and getting dressed completely wipe me out. My work is suffering to the point where I've had clients reassigned, and my wife has had to take over managing our household bills because I just can't keep up.\n\nDoctor: When you think it would be easier if you didn’t wake up, have you had any thoughts of killing yourself, made a plan, or done anything to prepare? Are you safe right now?\n\nPatient: I've definitely had those thoughts where I feel like everyone would be better off without me because I keep screwing things up, or that it wouldn't be so bad if I just didn't wake up. But I want to be clear that I haven't made any plans or taken any steps to hurt myself, and I am absolutely safe right now.\n\nDoctor: Even when you do get a chance to do something you usually enjoy, like car restoration or board-game nights, does it still feel hard to enjoy or care about it? Or do you think you would enjoy it normally if you had the time?\n\nPatient: I haven't really had the time or energy to try those things lately with everything going on. Because of that, I'm not completely sure if I would still enjoy them right now or not.\n\nDoctor: When something good happens or you get a brief break from dealing with your mom’s illness, does your mood lift noticeably, or do you still feel deeply depressed no matter what? Does the sadness feel mostly tied to that stress, or has it taken on a life of its own?\n\nPatient: Even when I get a break or spend time with my wife, my mood doesn't lift and I still feel that heavy, hollow sadness. While the stress with my mom started all of this, the sadness has definitely taken on a life of its own now and doesn't just go away when things quiet down.\n\nDoctor: Even when you do make yourself start something you used to enjoy, do you still get little or no pleasure from it? Can you think of a recent example?\n\nPatient: I honestly haven't even tried to start any of my hobbies lately because I just don't have the time or the energy. Because of that, I don't really have a recent example to share, and I'm not sure how I would feel doing them right now.\n\nDoctor: Over the past month, have you noticed a clear change in your appetite or weight, either eating much less or much more than usual? If yes, about how much has your weight changed?\n\nPatient: I haven't really noticed any significant changes in my appetite or weight recently. My mind has been so focused on my mother's health and all the stress at work that I haven't paid much attention to how much I'm eating.\n\nDoctor: Compared with what’s happening with your mother, does your depression feel clearly more intense than you would expect from that stress alone? Or does it feel about in proportion to the situation?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure, and I haven't really thought about it that way. Everything just feels incredibly overwhelming and hard to sort through right now, so it's difficult to tell."

Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities. Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns. Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Auditory Startle, Tactile Startle, Visual Startle, Slow Recovery\n[S081] Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor.  Subtypes: With Depressed Mood, With Anxiety, With Mixed Anxiety and Depressed Mood, With Disturbance of Conduct, With Mixed Disturbance of Emotions and Conduct, Unspecified\n[S082] Depersonalization_Derealization: Altered sense of the reality of one's self or surroundings after a traumatic event. Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge. Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort. Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety. Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns.  Subtypes: Mirror Checking or Avoidance, Excessive Camouflaging, Skin Picking, Reassurance Seeking, Comparing, Seeking Cosmetic Procedures\n[S069] Intrusive_Symptoms: Recurrent, involuntary, and intrusive distressing memories of the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Auditory Startle, Tactile Startle, Visual Startle, Slow Recovery\n[S081] Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor.  Subtypes: With Depressed Mood, With Anxiety, With Mixed Anxiety and Depressed Mood, With Disturbance of Conduct, With Mixed Disturbance of Emotions and Conduct, Unspecified\n[S082] Depersonalization_Derealization: Altered sense of the reality of one's self or surroundings after a traumatic event.
